# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-11-07

## Embodied AI
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Open-HumanWAM: An Open Recipe for Scaling Human and Robot Experience for Embodied Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06817v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Egocentric human video offers a scalable source of manipulation priors for robot learning, yet translating raw data scale into generalizable robot policies demands rigorous data curation and training recipes. We introduce Open-HumanWAM, the first fully open recipe for scaling egocentric pretraining to 50,000 hours, anchored by Open-HumanWAM-50K---a curated egocentric video corpus densely annotated with hand motion and natural language. Leveraging this corpus, we systematically investigate pure human pretraining via closed-loop simulation evaluations and uncover an empirical log-linear scaling law linking pretraining data hours to downstream robot success rates. Grounded in these findings, we develop HumanWAM-Vanilla, a world--action model pretrained exclusively on 50k hours of egocentric human video. Upon robot post-training, HumanWAM-Vanilla reaches 92.08% success on RoboTwin 2.0 Clean2Clean and 68.88% on Clean2Random, alongside a 14.17% mean success rate and a 19.00 mean progress score on RoboDojo. To push the capability frontier further, we explore joint human--robot pretraining with HumanWAM-Goal, a visual-goal-conditioned world--action model. Hybrid data scaling unlocks broad zero-shot generalization across both simulation and physical benchmarks, propelling post-trained RoboTwin 2.0 performance to new state-of-the-art levels: 92.70% on Clean2Clean and a substantial leap to 84.60% on Clean2Random. On physical robots, HumanWAM-Goal further exhibits emergent embodied in-context learning, dynamically adapting its manipulation behavior to unseen visual goals without parameter updates. To support reproducible scaling research in embodied AI, we open-source the Open-HumanWAM-50K corpus, data curation and pretraining pipelines, alongside the pretrained and post-trained HumanWAM checkpoints, inference code, and simulation evaluation suites.</p></details> | <details><summary>GitHu...</summary><p>GitHub: https://github.com/DAGroup-PKU/HumanNet/tree/Open-HumanWAM Project website: https://dagroup-pku.github.io/open-humanwam/</p></details> |
| **[NeoWorld-Pro: Programming Interactive Scenes from Monocular Images for Embodied Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24212v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The advancement of Embodied AI necessitates high-quality simulation assets that faithfully mirror the real world. However, transforming raw visual observations into simulation-ready scenes remains challenging due to the lack of physical grounding and scene-level interactivity in current image-to-URDF methods. We propose NeoWorld-Pro, a framework that reformulates monocular scene reconstruction as procedural programming for interactive 3D environments. Leveraging the zero-shot reasoning and code synthesis capabilities of MLLMs, NeoWorld-Pro converts a single RGB image into executable programs specifying object geometry, articulation, and physical properties. A physics-in-the-loop mechanism then iteratively refines the generated programs by validating their execution in a physics engine, enforcing physically plausible articulations, valid object compositions and interactions, and accurate spatial relationships. Experiments show that NeoWorld-Pro outperforms open-loop and prior monocular reconstruction methods, while enabling complex downstream tasks such as stable stacking and fine-grained manipulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://neoworldproject.github.io/neoworld-pro-website/</p></details> |
| **[EgoKit: Towards Unified Low-Cost Egocentric Data Collection with Heterogeneous Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.16797v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Egocentric video is increasingly used as a data source for robot learning, activity understanding, and embodied AI research, but collecting it at scale remains fragmented in practice: each candidate host device, such as an Android phone, iPhone, iPad, smart glasses, or extended reality (XR) headset, exposes a different SDK, a different policy on raw camera access, and different limitations on external USB cameras and on-device tracking. Synchronized ego-view and wrist-view capture is therefore typically obtained by either committing to a single proprietary platform or building one-off rigs that do not transfer across devices. To address this gap, we present EgoKit, a toolkit that exposes the same egocentric recording workflow across six heterogeneous host devices. Across all supported devices, EgoKit presents the same recording interaction and produces locally stored video with a uniform log format; on XR headsets, it additionally logs head pose and OpenXR-standard 26-joint hand tracking aligned to the video streams. The companion accessories, including two wrist cameras with mounts, a head strap, and a USB-C hub, add wrist-view capture to any supported host without custom hardware fabrication. To validate that EgoKit data can train robot policies, we replicated HumanEgo with 41 demonstrations recorded by EgoKit-PICO: in simulation, policies trained on 20 or 40 of them placed a cube on a tray in 60-74% of 100 unseen layouts, about as often as policies trained on Project Aria recordings of the same layouts. EgoKit is available at https://egokit.chuange.org/.</p></details> |  |
| **[PanoWorld: Geometry-Consistent Panoramic Video World Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15391v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present PanoWorld, a panoramic video world model that generates geometry-consistent 360$\degree$ video from a single image and a caption. Existing panoramic video methods optimize primarily for visual realism and do not explicitly constrain the underlying 3D scene state, producing outputs that appear plausible yet exhibit inconsistent depth, broken correspondences, and implausible motion across the spherical surface. We address this gap by framing panoramic video generation as a geometry- and dynamics-consistent latent state modeling problem rather than pure visual synthesis. Building on a pre-trained perspective video world model, we introduce two lightweight regularizers: a depth consistency loss against pseudo ground-truth panoramic depth, and a trajectory consistency loss that supervises the 3D world-frame positions of tracked points across time. We further apply spherical-geometry-aware adaptation to the conditioning and positional encoding. We additionally introduce PanoGeo, a unified geometry-aware panoramic video dataset with consistent depth, trajectory, and prompt annotations across diverse real and synthetic sources, used for both training and stratified evaluation. Experiments show that PanoWorld improves geometric consistency over prior panoramic generation methods while maintaining competitive visual realism, establishing that panoramic video generation must be treated as a geometric modeling problem to support the holistic spatial understanding requirements of embodied AI applications. Code is available at https://github.com/ostadabbas/PanoWorld.</p></details> |  |
| **[OdorSpaces: Visual-Olfactory Embodied Navigation in 3D Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05738v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Olfaction provides critical information about airborne chemical cues, yet it remains largely underexplored in embodied AI, with limited prior machine olfaction work focusing primarily on semantic odor perception. We introduce OdorSpaces, a physics-based simulation platform and benchmark for visual-olfactory embodied navigation. OdorSpaces augments Habitat-Sim with 3D odor transport and dispersion simulated by Fire Dynamics Simulator (FDS), enabling agents to navigate photorealistic indoor environments using both visual and olfactory observations. The benchmark includes two representative source-seeking scenarios: invisible gas-leak localization and early-stage fire localization. We develop an initial multimodal reinforcement learning policy that integrates visual observations and olfactory measurements over time. Experiments show that the policy outperforms a diverse set of baselines, establishing a strong reference for future research on visual-olfactory embodied navigation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 10th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL 2026). 20 pages, 4 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |
| **[Embodied AI for Scientific Discovery: From Physical Action to Scientific Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04371v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific experiments are increasingly selected, executed and interpreted by learned systems that act through physical interfaces. Robot learning builds physical competence, whereas AI for science chooses which experiment to run, each reporting different endpoints. This review treats their interface as one problem, embodied AI for science, where the experiment itself is partially observed: specimen state, apparatus state and measurement processes are latent, actions are costly and irreversible, and missingness depends on execution and outcome. We organize the literature by three closures operating at distinct levels: operational closure (the experiment realized is the one intended), epistemic closure (the result updates beliefs or selects subsequent experiments) and evidential closure (the record supports a claim). We analyze workflows through a record-linked evidence cycle - Record, Diagnose, Calibrate, Confirm - as analytic functions with feedback. A taxonomy crosses these closures with six delegated decisions and five embodiment classes. Our formulation separates commanded, realized and observed conditions and models diagnosis with explicit resource, risk and sample-change costs. We compare experiment-selection methods by terminal decision, review perception, protocol compilation, visuomotor policies, world models, recovery and transfer by evidence emission requirements, and compare chemistry, materials, biology, microscopy and field workflows by what their records establish. An appendix provides an evidence map of 28 workflows (26 physical, two software-boundary), characterizing validation pathways and claim confirmation requirements. We propose evaluation that separates actions, valid experiments, campaigns and confirmed claims, framing open challenges as controlled comparisons with measurable failure conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>46 pa...</summary><p>46 pages, 10 figures, 14 tables. Review article</p></details> |
| **[RoboScope Kit: Unified Interfaces for Fast and Scalable Embodied-AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03850v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Simulation benchmarks are the standard way to compare robot policies, but each benchmark brings its own evaluator, observation layout, and action convention, and evaluation is slow: 500 RoboTwin Easy episodes with one simulator and one model on one GPU take 359.7 minutes. Profiling the official StarVLA evaluator shows that simulation occupies CPU cores while the GPU averages 14.3% utilization. RoboScope Kit wraps each model once in a Python class called a Policy, and each benchmark once in an adapter around the benchmark's own evaluator, so the benchmark's rules and scoring stay unchanged. Scheduling runs simulator workers and Policy replicas as separate pools, sizes both from measured probes, and shards work deterministically across GPU instances; runtime optimization renders only the observations an action-chunk Policy reads. Adapters cover six benchmark families. On LIBERO-Long with a StarVLA checkpoint on one 48 GiB RTX 4090, the concurrency search selects twelve simulator workers sharing three Policy replicas, which evaluate 500 episodes 4.45$\times$ faster than the official StarVLA evaluation with equivalent success rates, 2.78$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 1.52$\times$ faster than vla-eval at its tuned configuration. With CUDA MPS, which lets the replicas run their kernels concurrently, the search selects sixteen workers sharing four replicas, 6.16$\times$ faster than StarVLA, 3.85$\times$ faster than RLinf, and 2.10$\times$ faster than vla-eval. On RoboTwin Easy, four GPUs with chunk-boundary observation complete the 500 episodes in 14.4 minutes, 25.04$\times$ faster than the single-GPU baseline, with identical success outcomes and terminal steps.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 9 figures. Project page: https://roboscope.pages.dev/ Code: https://github.com/MINT-SJTU/RoboScope-Kit</p></details> |
| **[Labference: Benchmarking the Reasoning-Action Connection in Robotic Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.35252v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realizing Embodied AI for Science requires robots to translate experimental goals into reliable physical procedures, reducing hazardous and repetitive human work while generating evidence for scientific discovery. Although language-based planning and learned manipulation provide complementary capabilities, their integration requires robots to adapt subsequent operations to actual execution outcomes. For example, an unsuccessful placement changes what must be done next, even when the experimental goal remains unchanged. Existing benchmarks assess planning, manipulation, and laboratory workflows through task completion and reasoning or execution metrics; however, aggregate performance leaves the connection between outcome-dependent choices and physical execution unresolved. To address this evaluation gap, we introduce Labference, a benchmark for evaluating how observed physical outcomes guide subsequent choices and how those choices translate into robotic laboratory actions. Its evaluation protocol is designed to test outcome-dependent behavioral adaptation through controlled evidence variations, while independently measuring operation selection, physical execution, and task completion. Matched Oracle-assisted continuations from shared checkpoints then assess which forms of support improve subsequent choices and task completion. Initial weighing evaluations reveal target-selection errors in the tested VLA policies despite successful object transfers, whereas GPT-driven control achieves correct selection but encounters execution failures and substantial computation costs. Together, these findings highlight the need to connect task-appropriate reasoning with reliable, timely action. By making this connection measurable and diagnosable, Labference supports targeted improvements toward autonomous experimentation for Embodied AI for Science.</p></details> | <details><summary>Updat...</summary><p>Update project address</p></details> |
| **[When the Past Shapes Action: Understanding Embodied Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41688v1)** | 2026-10-30 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent advances in generative foundation models have expanded the capabilities of embodied AI, enabling increasingly general navigation, manipulation, and long-horizon task execution. However, effective interaction with dynamic environments requires more than only strong perception and reasoning: agents must act with partial knowledge and retain, retrieve, and update information from past observations, actions, and experiences. This survey examines embodied memory as a mechanism for preserving decision-relevant information beyond the current observation. We organize existing approaches along two complementary dimensions: six fundamental memory requirements, spanning world-state persistence, task progress, belief maintenance, and experiential learning; and eight architectural categories, including recurrent states, fast weights, visual and symbolic representations, spatial memory, and experience-based systems. We analyze their capabilities and limitations, and examine how existing benchmarks evaluate memory-dependent behavior, distinguishing genuine use of historical information from general task performance. Finally, we identify open challenges in selecting relevant experiences, maintaining consistent beliefs in changing environments, and consolidating knowledge across episodes. Our analysis provides a framework for understanding memory as an essential component of reliable, adaptive, and lifelong-horizon embodied intelligence.</p></details> |  |
| **[Gym-Anything for Robotics: Scaling physical simulations with digital agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40565v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Realistic, fast robotics simulations could enable large-scale training of embodied AI models that transfer to the real world. However, despite decades of progress, available simulations cover only a narrow range of real-world tasks and physical phenomena. The core bottleneck is that creating better simulations requires significant expert effort and therefore does not scale. To address this, we introduce Gym-Anything for Robotics, a framework in which coding agents create realistic robotics simulations. Our core insight is that simulation creation is a coding agent task, so improvements in coding agents translate directly into richer and more faithful simulations. We select simulations based on U.S. GDP data to cover economically valuable physical work, define what makes a good simulation and a good task, and develop an adversarial creation-audit loop that checks each simulation and task against these definitions. Using this pipeline, we build RoboWorld, a set of 25 simulations with 25 test and 175 training RL environments of varying difficulty. Even the best frontier models fail on every RoboWorld test task, achieving an average reward of only 0.06. Building on RoboWorld, we introduce several new ways for frontier models to improve, including in-context learning across repeated attempts, a privileged teacher that reads the simulator state and teaches a student in context, self-assisted learning from the model's own privileged experience, and code policies that generate demonstrations across varied scenes to train vision-language-action models (VLAs) such as $π_{0.5}$. Each of these substantially improves performance. We release our pipeline, RL environments, and benchmark to facilitate future research. Code and data are available at https://gym-anything.github.io/robotics</p></details> |  |
| **[UniffordInteractor: Reasoning the Unified Affordance for Human and Multi-Object Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39745v1)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deriving plausible and dynamic Human Object Interaction (HOI) has gained tremendous progress in Embodied AI and Immersive AR/VR. While existing HOI methods have effectively explored human interaction with individual or fixed objects, they still show limitations in complex multi-object interaction scenes. Meanwhile, previous approaches focus more on the contact information held by human-object relation, overlooking the significance of the contact situation of various entities (i.e., object-object). To address these limitations, we propose UniffordInteractor, a Reasoning-then-Interacting framework that leverages unified affordance for realistic and long-horizon human and multi-object interaction modeling. At the reasoning stage, we devise a Unified Affordance Reasoner (UAR) that employs a fine-tuned 3D Multimodal Large Language Model for unified object-centric affordance prediction. At the interacting stage, we propose Hierarchical Interaction Generator (HIG), which formulates the interaction generation as a hierarchical (e.g., root to body to hand to objects) autoregressive prediction, conditioned on the obtained unified affordance. Extensive experiments demonstrate that UniffordInteractor surpasses SOTA counterparts in human and multi-object interaction generation.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 11 figures. Published in ACM Transactions on Graphics (Proceedings of SIGGRAPH Asia 2026)</p></details> |
| **[OmniDex: Scaling Dexterous Hand Grasping to Diverse Cluttered Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11194v2)** | 2026-10-29 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dexterous grasping is the foundational primitive in embodied AI, demanding massive data to train robust models. As real-world data collection is expensive, simulation has become the mainstream paradigm. Yet, while cluttered scenes best reflect real-world applications, learning to grasp within them is bottlenecked by a critical scarcity of large-scale data. To resolve this, we curate high-quality 3D objects and supporting bases, proposing a scalable seed-and-filter strategy that bypasses sluggish scene-level optimization. This yields an unprecedented benchmark comprising over 2.6 million scenes and 0.4B scene-specific grasp ground truths, featuring diverse realistic layouts paired with rich semantic and geometric observations. Furthermore, we introduce the OmniDex model to overcome the grasp multimodality and last-millimeter precision errors plaguing current generative models. By coupling Soft Winner-Takes-All learning with human-inspired physical constraints during training, and utilizing physics-driven ranking, our approach achieves robust dexterous grasping without the latency of post-optimization. Experimental results show that OmniDex model achieves state-of-the-art performance and strong generalization across diverse scenes, views, and unseen objects.</p></details> |  |
| **[InHabit: Scaling 3D Human-Scene Interaction Data With Image Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.19673v3)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI and human-centric 3D scene understanding require data of people meaningfully interacting with diverse environments, yet such data remains scarce. Real-world capture has steadily pushed toward more scenes, but is bound to studios and at most around a hundred scenes, while synthetic datasets scale easily but place people without regard to what the scene affords. We argue that useful human-scene interaction data requires diversity in both scenes and interactions, and we show this with our proposed data engine InHabit. InHabit is a fully automatic engine that renders an existing 3D scene, uses 2D foundation models to propose and depict context-appropriate interactions, and lifts them into SMPL-X bodies, where the known scene geometry makes lifting well-constrained. Applied to Habitat-Matterport3D, InHabit produces InHabitants, which pairs photorealistic images with SMPL-X bodies and complete scene geometry across ~6.6k rooms, about 60x more scenes than the largest captured dataset. We illustrate the quality and value of such large-scale human-scene interaction data through a variety of experiments. In a perceptual study, our interactions are preferred over two prior placement methods in 78% of cases. Across three tasks - 1) contact estimation, 2) human-scene reconstruction, and 3) generating humans in scenes - training with InHabitants outperforms training on synthetic data with exact ground truth (BEDLAM), motion-capture data (TRUMANS), and real RGB-D captures (PROX); e.g., GRAFT trained on InHabitants reaches a contact F1 of 0.594 on PROX, versus 0.492 when trained on PROX itself. Project page: https://virtualhumans.mpi-inf.mpg.de/inhabit/</p></details> |  |
| **[POD-RoPE: Rotary Position Encoding over Physical Observation Distributions for Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33745v2)** | 2026-10-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied perception requires positional representations that capture both physical geometry and the spatial and temporal extent of observations. We introduce POD-RoPE (Physical Observation Distribution RoPE), which represents token observation footprints as distributions over camera centers, viewing directions, and sampling times. Under independent sampling, expected rotations encode relative relationships between distributions; when each distribution collapses to a single address, the construction recovers pointwise RoPE. The same rotation statistics support Q/K matching and coherence-dependent V/O transformations, allowing observation geometry to guide both information selection and content aggregation. Per-token pre-aggregation avoids increasing sequence length or processing pairs of observation nodes. We evaluate POD-RoPE across four robot policy architectures, simulation and real-world manipulation, and complementary reconstruction, depth-estimation, and novel-view-synthesis tasks. Integrating POD-RoPE into Fast-WAM improves mean success from 91.83% to 92.88% across all 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks, and from 32.5% to 52.5% on two real-robot tasks on Sharpa North. Controlled reconstruction ablations support the contributions of distribution encoding and V/O transformations. In H20 model-block benchmarks, POD-RoPE achieves median forward-plus-backward speedups of $1.91\times$ over P-RoPE and $5.34\times$ over RayRoPE, with a median latency overhead of 17.4% over Standard RoPE. These results support physical observation distributions as an effective and computationally efficient basis for geometry-aware attention.</p></details> | 21 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Zooming without Zooming v2: From Seeing Details to Connecting Evidence for Generalizable Multimodal Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37037v1)** | 2026-10-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal perception forms the foundation of multimodal intelligence. In particular, spatial perception and multimodal long-context understanding are two pivotal tasks that underpin embodied AI and multimodal agents. However, synthesizing targeted, high-quality training data for these tasks remains difficult and costly. Our key insight is that these tasks share an underlying capability, which we call connecting evidence: identifying relevant visual facts, binding them to the correct entities, recovering their relations, and composing them into an answer. Crucially, targeted training data are far easier to synthesize for this capability than for these tasks themselves. To strengthen this capability, we propose EvidenceConnect, an easy-to-implement data synthesis framework that uses zoomed views of related regions within a single image to generate questions requiring comparison, aggregation, or chaining of visual evidence. To ensure quality, a reference answer is retained only when independent VLMs agree on it, and region-ablation tests discard questions whose answer survives the removal of selected regions. We then train base VLMs on the resulting data with RLVR, obtaining Zooming without Zooming v2 (ZwZ2). Although the synthesized data are not tailored to any downstream benchmark, ZwZ2 improves fine-grained and spatial perception, with further gains on embodied spatial reasoning, multimodal long-context understanding, multimodal agent tasks, and general visual reasoning. These results establish EvidenceConnect as an effective framework for supervising evidence connection and suggest that strengthening this shared capability leads to more generalizable multimodal perception.</p></details> |  |
| **[AmaraSpatial-10K: A Spatially and Semantically Aligned 3D Dataset for Spatial Computing and Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.23018v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Web-scale 3D asset collections are abundant but rarely ready to ingest without cleanup, suffering from arbitrary metric scaling, incorrect pivots, brittle geometry, and incomplete textures, defects that limit their use in embodied AI, robotics, and spatial computing. We present AmaraSpatial-10K, a dataset of 10,071 synthetic 3D assets standardised for direct ingestion. Each asset ships as a metric-scaled, deterministically anchored .glb with separated PBR maps, a convex collision hull, a paired reference image, and multi-sentence text metadata. Alongside the dataset we introduce a reusable evaluation suite for 3D asset banks, a continuous Scale Plausibility Score (SPS), a keyword-based Concept Density metric, anchor-error auditing, and a cross-modal CLIP coherence protocol, and apply it to AmaraSpatial-10K alongside matched subsets of Objaverse, HSSD, ABO, and GSO. We check the annotations independently of the LLM/VLM pipeline that produced them: measured ABO object heights fall inside our scale intervals 88.7-90.7% of the time, and three non-author raters auditing a stratified sample of 314 assets judge 95.5% plausibly scaled and 99.7% correctly anchored. AmaraSpatial-10K improves CLIP Recall@5 by 3.4x over Objaverse (0.612 vs. 0.181, median rank 267 -> 3), achieves a 99.1% settling-stability rate under Habitat-Sim in static placement and collision tests, with ~20x lower per-load wall time, and as a drop-in Holodeck asset bank improves planned-scale matching and is preferred by blinded human raters in 27 of 40 paired scenes. Controlled ablations on the same asset bank attribute the retrieval gain to description richness.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to NeurIPS 2026 (Evaluations and Datasets Track)</p></details> |
| **[Closing the empirical loop: autonomous AI agents conduct end-to-end research with human participants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13421v3)** | 2026-10-26 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Artificial intelligence systems are transforming scientific discovery by accelerating specific research tasks, from protein structure prediction to materials design, yet remain confined to narrow domains requiring substantial human oversight. Exponential growth of scientific literature and increasing domain specialisation constrain researchers capacity to synthesise knowledge across disciplines and develop unifying theories, motivating exploration of more general-purpose AI systems for science. Here we show that a domain-agnostic, agentic AI system can independently navigate the scientific workflow, from hypothesis generation through data collection to manuscript preparation. The system autonomously designed and executed three psychological studies on visual working memory, mental rotation, and imagery vividness, executed online data collection with 288 participants, developed analysis pipelines through over 8-hour continuous coding sessions, and produced completed manuscripts. The results demonstrate the capability of AI scientific discovery pipelines to conduct research with theoretical reasoning and methodological rigour comparable to experienced researchers, though with limitations in conceptual nuance and theoretical interpretation. This is a step toward embodied AI that can test hypotheses through real-world experiments, accelerating discovery by autonomously exploring regions of scientific space that human cognitive and resource constraints might otherwise leave unexplored. It raises important questions about the nature of scientific understanding and attribution of scientific credit.</p></details> |  |
| **[EAST: Embodied AI Safety Testing via Systematic Fault Injection in Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34358v1)** | 2026-10-25 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Task success is not a sufficient measure of safety for embodied artificial intelligence (AI), because robots may complete objectives while violating safety requirements. Existing evaluations often emphasize performance and robustness, but lack a systematic route from documented safety hazards to executable, verifiable tests. We present EAST (Embodied AI Safety Testing), a digital-twin framework that converts literature-grounded safety evidence into fault-injection tests across perception, instructions, environments, actions, and timing. EAST combines an expert-reviewed safety-fault taxonomy and eight executable operators with applicability and validity constraints. Its three-stage workflow generates test specifications, verifies fault realization during simulated execution, and independently assesses safety challenges, violations, task outcomes, and behavioral signatures using simulator-state evidence. The framework preserves execution traces and replayable records, supporting attribution and reproducibility across experiments. We evaluate EAST on six RoboTwin manipulation tasks and two policy architectures, ACT and RDT, against alternative testing strategies under matched budgets. Across five comparative campaigns, EAST targets all 74 admissible task-policy-operator combinations and obtains valid evidence for 63, while achieving safety-challenge exposure rates close to Random-Legal and Rule-Based. Moreover, 82 valid EAST executions complete their tasks despite safety violations, revealing risks concealed by success-oriented evaluation. These findings support systematic, validity-aware, reproducible safety testing beyond task-completion metrics.</p></details> |  |
| **[3D Generation for Embodied AI and Robotic Simulation: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26509v4)** | 2026-10-23 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI and robotic systems increasingly depend on scalable, diverse, and physically grounded 3D content for simulation-based training and real-world deployment. While 3D generative modeling has advanced rapidly, embodied applications impose requirements far beyond visual realism: generated objects must carry kinematic structure and material properties, scenes must support interaction and task execution, and the resulting content must bridge the gap between simulation and reality. This survey reviews 3D generation for embodied AI and organizes the literature around three roles that 3D generation plays in embodied systems. In Data Generator, 3D generation produces simulation-ready objects and assets, including articulated, physically grounded, and deformable content for downstream interaction; in Simulation Environments, it constructs interactive and task-oriented worlds, spanning structure-aware, controllable, and agentic scene generation; and in Sim2Real Bridge, it supports digital twin reconstruction, data augmentation, and synthetic demonstrations for downstream robot learning and real-world transfer. We also show that the field is shifting from visual realism toward interaction readiness, and we identify the main bottlenecks, including limited physical annotations, the gap between geometric quality and physical validity, fragmented evaluation, and the persistent sim-to-real divide, that must be addressed for 3D generation to become a dependable foundation for embodied intelligence. Our project page is at https://3dgen4robot.github.io.</p></details> | <details><summary>27 pa...</summary><p>27 pages. For paper updates, please refer to https://3dgen4robot.github.io</p></details> |
| **[rMuscle: Robotic Muscle Memory for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Model Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19104v3)** | 2026-10-21 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Factory work is a promising early scenario for embodied AI: assigning repetitive manual jobs to robots has clear economic payoff, and a structured station keeps the jobs tractable for current policies. Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models now dominate as the policy paradigm for these robots. The inference latency of VLA models directly affects robot responsiveness and motion smoothness. However, existing VLA inference frameworks do not fully exploit the characteristics of embodied workloads or account for the distinct bottlenecks across different stages of VLA inference. In this paper, we first characterize embodied workloads and identify substantial task similarity across repeated robot executions. We further find that such similarity extends beyond observations and action trajectories to internal model states. Drawing on these observations, we present rMuscle, a real-time VLA inference framework inspired by human muscle memory. It exploits cross-execution similarity through a dual-phase muscle-memory cache. The Context Cache reuses visual-token outputs to reduce computation, while the Action Cache reuses neuron activation patterns to reduce weight accesses. We keep both the cache memory footprint and access overhead low through online cache recomputation, sliding-window cache retrieval, and mask sharing across consecutive denoising steps. rMuscle achieves 1.29-1.42X speedup on RTX 4090 and Jetson Thor across LIBERO, RoboTwin, and physical manipulation tasks, while maintaining the original success rates on real-world robots.</p></details> |  |
| **[World2HOSI: A Multi-Expert Harness for Long-Horizon Human-Object-Scene Interaction Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27725v1)** | 2026-10-20 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon human-object-scene interaction (HOSI) is central to embodied AI, animation, and game development. Given a shared, structured scene state and an orchestration harness grounded in it, existing experts can jointly accomplish tasks beyond the reach of any of them alone. A harness plans action stages over this state, invokes experts via their native conditioning interfaces, and writes each action's effects back, so that every invocation is grounded in the evolving world. To reduce physical artifacts, bounded residual optimization and contact-driven physics simulation further ensure the physical plausibility of human motion and object manipulation, respectively, while preserving interaction intent. | **[Physical AI: Concepts, Techniques, and Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27045v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In recent years, physical artificial intelligence (physical AI) has experienced rapid development, capturing significant attention in academia and industry. However, the field has yet to reach consensus on its definition, technical landscape, and core challenges. To address this gap, this monograph seeks to define physical AI, present its technical landscape, and examine each key technical subfield in depth. In doing so, it establishes a unified framework for Physical AI: artificial systems that perceive, model, and act upon the physical world through continuous feedback under real-world constraints. Further, this monograph distinguishes physical AI from conventional digital AI, embodied AI, cyber-physical systems, and digital twins. It develops the foundations of physical AI across physics, control, cognition, learning, and reasoning. It reviews key methodologies, including multimodal perception, physical data and simulation, world models, embodied foundation models, motor-control systems, multi-agent coordination, and hardware-software co-design. Furthermore, it examines applications spanning robotics, manufacturing, infrastructure, scientific experimentation, and autonomous services. Particular attention is given to sim-to-real transfer, reliability, real-time execution, evaluation, safety, governance, and long-term deployment. By connecting perception, prediction, decision-making, action, and adaptation in a closed loop, this monograph identifies the technical challenges and research directions required to advance from narrow automation toward more generalizable physical AI and, ultimately, physical artificial general intelligence (Physical AGI).</p></details> | <details><summary>Monog...</summary><p>Monograph, 12 chapters, 388 pages, 64 figures</p></details> |
| **[ForagerSim: Evaluating Embodied Map-Based Reasoning in Large-Scale Dynamic Worlds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26395v1)** | 2026-10-19 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Animals build cognitive maps of vast environments, use them to perform map-based navigation, and might even be reusing the same circuitry used in spatial reasoning for creating and using maps of arbitrary task-spaces. To address this, we present ForagerSim --- an interdisciplinary research platform and simulation environment focused on embodied tasks that require map-based reasoning, with scenarios ranging from mobile-robot search and rescue to animal foraging in both complex mazes and vast, potentially infinite open areas. ForagerSim is highly modular and allows modifying independent axes of difficulty related to perception (abstract 2D sensing to full RGB or LiDAR), scale (room-scale to multi-km scale), topology (open-fields to complex mazes), dynamics (smoke, debris, sensorimotor failures and others) and tasks (from simple 2D coverage to foraging-inspired well-alternation tasks). | **[Compile Once, Run Cheap: Frozen, Portable Skills for Embodied Agents, and What Recursive Self-Improvement Adds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25114v1)** | 2026-10-18 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Embodied AI lacks the data-rich pretraining that gave language models their agents and skills, so new manipulation capability costs demonstrations per task or a frontier model in every episode. We call this skill-level recursive self-improvement Skill-RSI and specify a compile protocol (isolated, audited author; hidden validation layouts; no-feedback and inherit-only controls; tamper-evident ledger) and a reuse protocol that runs the identical files with executors from another model family. Planning toward this goal is then organized as a graph of rule-specific nodes, where higher-level nodes inherit the union of their parents' rules, and candidate plans are progressively verified and aggregated until a single plan satisfies every relevant rule. | **[Generative AI for Autonomous Driving: Frontiers and Opportunities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.08854v2)** | 2026-10-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) constitutes a transformative technological wave that reconfigures industries through its unparalleled capabilities for content creation, reasoning, planning, and multimodal understanding. We begin by distilling the principles and trade-offs of modern generative modeling, encompassing VAEs, GANs, Diffusion Models, and Large Language Models (LLMs). We identify key obstacles and possibilities such as comprehensive generalization across rare cases, evaluation and safety checks, budget-limited implementation, regulatory compliance, ethical concerns, and environmental effects, while proposing research plans across theoretical assurances, trust metrics, transport integration, and socio-technical influence. | **[WorldAttention: An Efficient Attention Architecture for Interactive Video World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34606v1)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Leveraging the paradigm of autoregressive diffusion, text-conditioned interactive video world models aim to simulate temporally coherent environments guided by textual instructions. | **[A Comprehensive Review of Generative Physical Artificial Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.18111v1)** | 2026-09-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The integration of large-scale foundation models with physical embodiments has led to significant advancements in robotics known as Generative Physical Artificial Intelligence (GPAI). | **[Latent RL Priors for Data-Efficient Dexterous Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08458v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning dexterous manipulation from limited visual demonstrations is challenging because policies must acquire both perceptual grounding and precise control, while reinforcement learning (RL) in simulation can acquire rich control behaviors from large-scale interaction with privileged information. We introduce \textbf{Latent RL Prior}, a two-stage framework that reuses this control knowledge for data-efficient visual policy learning. During privileged pretraining, we distill large-scale RL rollouts into a flow-matching action decoder conditioned on proprioception and latent task context. During visual adaptation, we freeze the decoder and learn a visual interface that maps images to its latent context using action supervision alone, without privileged labels or latent targets. The same decoder can further be shared across arms by learning coordinated visual contexts for bimanual manipulation. Across three simulated tasks, our method outperforms full-parameter fine-tuning of $π_{0.5}$ and GR00T N1.7 in the low-data regime; on door lifting and bimanual assembly, it surpasses full-data baselines using $10\times$ and $5\times$ fewer demonstrations, respectively. Further analysis shows that direct visual-to-latent conditioning improves adaptation performance and training stability over routing visual predictions through the pretrained state encoder. Robustness to unseen mass variation and greater linear accessibility of contact-force information in decoder representations further provide evidence of retained RL-acquired control knowledge. Project website: https://yixiaowang7.github.io/latent-rl-prior-page/.</p></details> |  |

With an external post-trained teacher, OPD generally loses to GRPO in accuracy per training FLOP at matched settings, even when the teacher is further trained with GRPO, and often suffers response-length inflation. Yet a different teacher changes the picture: when the teacher is the student's own GRPO checkpoint, OPD recovers that checkpoint's accuracy on math with $50\times$ fewer distillation FLOPs than GRPO needs to reach the same accuracy. Following the same teacher setting, we consistently observe this high efficiency with a larger student, on code, and in a second model family. On a single domain, however, the GRPO checkpoint is already the desired model, so distilling it adds to total compute. Across domains, separate GRPO checkpoints hold different capabilities, and no single checkpoint serves every domain. Multi-teacher OPD (DeepSeek-AI, 2026) consolidates per-domain GRPO experts into a single student that matches each expert's accuracy, with the distillation stage costing only 5.2% (on 2 domains) and 8.0% (on 4 domains) of the specialist training FLOPs. A quadratic multi-index model updated via LoRA unveils the underlying mechanism: group-mean baseline subtraction tilts the balance between two competing forces, coherent fitting and gradient noise, toward spectral concentration and faster subspace rotation, while forcing the risk-favorable learning rate to be low. Guided by these dynamics, we tailor LoRA updates to each stage: in SFT, we flatten the spectrum of the full update to spread adaptation across singular directions, whereas in RL, we flatten the part of the update outside the current LoRA increment span to promote subspace rotation. In single-agent reinforcement learning, update rules discovered by meta-learning have achieved state-of-the-art results (e.g. DiscoRL). For each sample, its SAE activations indicate which regions it engages, and its gradients projected onto SAE features indicate how training on it would change these regions. All four models are open source: https://github.com/nace-ai/drex-decision-models</p></details> | <details><summary>51 pa...</summary><p>51 pages, 13 figures, 25 tables. | **[HyperGraphPro: Progress-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Structure-Guided Hypergraph RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.17755v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-hop question answering requires retrieval agents to construct a connected evidence path across reasoning turns rather than retrieve isolated relevant evidence for a single query. | **[Machine Learning Algorithms for Improving Black Box Optimization Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.25592v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Black-box optimization (BBO) addresses problems where objectives are accessible only through costly queries without gradients or explicit structure. | **[Provably Safe, Yet Scalable Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.14536v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safe reinforcement learning (RL) aims to learn policies that optimize rewards while satisfying constraints. Predominant approaches rely on soft-constrained policy optimization, which has achieved empirical success but does not provide formal safety guarantees for the learned policy. In contrast, methods with strict guarantees typically rely on explicit certificate functions, whose construction requires the direct synthesis and verification of control-invariant sets, a process that scales poorly with state dimension and often yields overly conservative behavior. In this paper, we present the Provably Safe, yet Scalable RL (PS2-RL) framework, a novel two-phase architecture for learning provably safe policies in a scalable manner, designed to overcome the key bottlenecks of prior methods. Rather than explicitly computing invariant sets, PS2-RL leverages a learned backup policy to forward-integrate the system dynamics, generating an implicit control-invariant set online. In the first phase, the backup policy is trained with the safe-arrival value function to achieve rapid, safe recovery to the certified base set for invariant-set construction. In the second phase, an RL policy is trained end-to-end through a differentiable projection layer that strictly enforces the safety guarantees induced by the learned backup policy. By maximizing the volume of the implicit control-invariant set in the first phase, the resulting PS2 policy from the second phase is performant and scalable, while maintaining provable safety. Crucially, PS2-RL imposes no restrictions on the underlying RL algorithm and can be plugged into any existing training pipeline. We establish theoretical guarantees for the proposed framework and evaluate it on robotic control tasks with state dimensions up to 10, a regime in which prior provably safe RL methods struggle or become impractical.</p></details> |  |

| **[TANGO: RNA Topology and Geometry Co-Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20277v3)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing RNA sequences that satisfy target tertiary-structure objectives is an important problem in RNA engineering, with broad applications in synthetic biology and therapeutics. | **[Reverse to Advance: Teleoperation-Cost Effective Hard Policy Learning from Reversed Easy Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13455v2)** | 2026-11-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>High-quality teleoperation datasets are costly to collect, particularly for hard tasks. We distill this supervision into a visual reward model. During real-world interaction, its predictions are combined with execution history to compute dense rewards for human-in-the-loop policy refinement. Across five real-world laboratory tasks, TwinBridge achieves a final mean autonomous success rate of 98% with at most 25 minutes of real-world interaction per task. Experiments show that dense rewards improve interaction efficiency over sparse rewards, and simulation-based policy initialization further accelerates learning.</p></details> | 8 pages, 6 figures |

| **[VisionSIM: Beyond Idealized Sensors - A Multi-Modal Simulator for Robot Perception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08368v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Robotic perception increasingly relies on heterogeneous sensor suites combining cameras with emerging modalities (event, SPAD, iToF/dToF, and thermal). We present VisionSIM, a unified, physically grounded simulation platform that elevates sensor realism to a first-class concern by modeling the raw physical measurement process for diverse conventional and emerging modalities. We expose fine-grained control over physical sensor parameters, reproducing failure modes overlooked by general-purpose simulators: pileup and glare for LiDAR; phase wrapping, saturation, and motion artifacts for iToF; refractory drops for event cameras; and history-dependent emission for thermal cameras. This enables principled cross-modality comparison on identical scenes and trajectories. We release the platform and a large indoor dataset with synchronized raw measurements and rich ground-truth annotations.</p></details> |  |

The model uses a transformer encoder-decoder leveraging rotary positional embeddings (RoPE) to generate a latent representation of trajectory-relevant and user-specific neuromotor factors, and augments trajectory prediction with flow matching to produce a distribution over plausible prescriptions biased toward the training distribution. Using data from 16 healthy participants completing 15 isometric trajectory tracking tasks, we evaluate generalization across held-out tasks and users, as well as the feasibility of user-specific fine-tuning within a therapy-session timescale. The model achieves mean trajectory prediction errors of <8% on held out tasks and users, with effectively instantaneous deterministic inference and sub-second sampling of trajectory distributions. An initial test using the model during live rehabilitation tasks demonstrates that, on average, participants reach the target neuromotor activations with just 15% error accumulated throughout the task. These results provide a proof of concept for data-driven, activation-targeted trajectory prescription in robot-mediated rehabilitation and establish a foundation for future validation in clinical populations. | **[H-TRIP: Exact Many-to-Many Distance Index for Multi-Robot Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08139v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Frontier-based exploration systems repeatedly evaluate geodesic travel costs between robot poses and candidate viewpoints, and tour-based planners consume the full pairwise distance matrix built from them. A strict visibility constraint guarantees connectivity, but a follower with no independent motion objective remains stationary, stalling the leader at the boundary of the safe set. Relaxing it preserves mobility at the cost of the guarantee. Our framework resolves this conflict through nested hard-soft visibility constraints: a full field-of-view (FoV) constraint enforced strictly to guarantee connectivity and a contracted FoV constraint imposed on each follower as a relaxed behavioral objective that drives it inward before the connectivity boundary is reached. We prove feasibility of the local quadratic programs and forward invariance of the connected, collision-free set under the resulting distributed controller. The contraction parameter shapes follower behavior without affecting the guarantee, which follows from the strict constraint alone. The framework is evaluated in confined and cluttered environments on two simulated systems: a kinematic integrator and software-in-the-loop Crazyflie quadrotors. Compared with a baseline tuned to reach the goal in comparable time, the framework keeps the links far closer to the connected set and moves the followers less.</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 4 figures, 1 table. Submitted to the American Control Conference (ACC)</p></details> |
| **[Haptic Shared Control for Multi-Robot Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05027v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent systems offer significant advantages in terms of flexibility and redundancy, enabling the efficient distribution of complex tasks among multiple robots. This algorithm, responsible for the autonomous behavior of the multi-robot system, is combined with different adaptive supervision strategies, allowing the user to exert control at varying scales over the entire group. (i) Single Agent Control (S): The operator directly controls a single robot, (ii) Pinning Control (P): The operator imposes constraints on multiple robots to influence the group, (iii) Centroid Control (C): The operator acts on the system's center of mass to steer the entire fleet. | **[ResponsibleRobotBench: Benchmarking Risk-Aware Task-Level Decision Making for Robotic Manipulation with Large Multimodal Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.04308v2)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safe task-level manipulation requires a planner to recognize hazards, select appropriate mitigation actions, and satisfy their execution prerequisites without abandoning the original goal. Correct hazard-category predictions also fail to ensure executable mitigation. In a cutting task under the default configuration, GPT-4o mini identifies the human hazard in all 100 episodes but never requests assistance, and every episode ends unsafe. Imitation-learning evaluations and physical robot sequences illustrate the need to coordinate mitigation with goal actions. Together, these results motivate evaluating mitigation selection, action prerequisites, and recovery alongside hazard recognition and terminal outcomes. | **[Online Hamilton-Jacobi Reachability Onboard the Robot Control Loop: A Real-Time GPU Solver for Safety-Critical Autonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04096v1)** | 2026-11-03 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Hamilton-Jacobi (HJ) reachability provides certified backward reachable tubes (BRTs) for safety-critical robotic systems, but the level-set computation scales exponentially in the state dimension and has historically been performed offline. Experiments on indoor and outdoor benchmarks show that CoReMatch strikes a strong balance between accuracy and efficiency, outperforming Efficient LoFTR in accuracy while running more than 10x faster on embedded edge hardware. These results demonstrate that recurrent coordinate refinement offers an efficient and scalable approach to real-time feature matching for robotic perception.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by CoRL 2026</p></details> |
| **[Bayesian Active Learning for Intent Disambiguation in Interactive Robot Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34270v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Interactive robot planning requires robots to infer and execute human intentions from natural language instructions that are often ambiguous, incomplete, or underspecified. Although large language models (LLMs) provide a powerful interface for clarification, relying on the generative model to drive an multi-turn conversation can introduce systematic failures. We propose a Bayesian framework that treats clarification as an active learning problem over grounded Signal Temporal Logic (STL) task specifications. Our method uses LLMs to initialize candidate formal specifications and translate informative contrasts into natural-language clarification questions, while Bayesian optimization maintains uncertainty estimation over user intent and selects queries that maximize information gain. After convergence, the inferred STL specification is passed to a formal planner to synthesize a verifiable robot trajectory. Across four simulated and real-world task domains, our approach generally achieves higher task satisfaction and requires fewer clarification rounds than LLM baselines, while helping smaller models close the performance gap against larger reasoning models.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by CoRL 2026 as Spotlight, also presented at IROS 2026 Human-Robot Dialogue workshop</p></details> |
| **[Pixel: A Co-Designed Full-Stack Robot System for Learning General-Purpose Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03648v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>General-purpose mobile manipulation requires a combination of capable robot hardware, scalable demonstration collection, expressive whole-body control, and effective policy learning. Yet these components are often developed independently, creating mismatches that limit the capabilities of the complete system. Pixel combines a low-cost wheeled humanoid with human-aligned kinematics and compliant grippers, multiple teleoperation and robot-free human data-collection interfaces, a unified whole-body controller, and a common policy learning pipeline across human and robot demonstrations. A hierarchical learning stack further enables language-conditioned visuomotor control and long-horizon task execution. We evaluate the system across hardware, data collection, and policy learning, and demonstrate mobile-manipulation behaviors requiring dexterity, active perception, navigation, and whole-body coordination. We hope that Pixel provides an integrated and extensible foundation for studying how robot hardware, data, control, and learning can be designed together for robot learning. More results at https://pixel-robot.com/pixel</p></details> | <details><summary>More ...</summary><p>More results at https://pixel-robot.com/pixel</p></details> |
| **[Robot-Matched Soft Fingers for UMI Data Collection: Lessons from 100 Hours of Pick and Place](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03779v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training robot policies for multi-task pick and place requires demonstrations on many objects, which handheld Universal Manipulation Interfaces (UMIs) can collect quickly and cheaply. We present a battery-powered handheld interface, built on YUBI, whose soft, angled fingers wrap around objects, deform visibly under load as a visual proxy for force, and match the grippers of the robot. With it, we collect a 99.7-hour dataset over 53 objects, train $π_{0.5}$ policies on 49 of them, and hold out the other 4 as unseen objects. We find that a top camera raises success by 13 points even when only about half of the data includes it, and that mid-training on our multi-task data raises success after fine-tuning on unseen objects from 35% to 57%. Finger design, dataset, and data processing code: https://lute-corp.github.io/soft-yubi-site/</p></details> | <details><summary>8 pag...</summary><p>8 pages, 3 figures, 3 tables. Accepted as a poster at the CoRL 2026 UMI Arena Workshop (Track 3). Project page: https://lute-corp.github.io/soft-yubi-site/</p></details> |
| **[Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.30613v5)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Changes to what a robot can see do not mean that there is a change in the problem it is trying to solve, and many language-conditioned manipulation systems struggle with this distinction. When something in a scene moves, the robot might need to execute its actions in different places, but its manipulation plan can stay the same. We propose Sequential Planning via Anchored Robotic Keypoints (SPARK), which describes a task as a symbolic behavior tree that we anchor to the current 3D observations when each action is called. By verifying the plan at runtime we can recover from failures locally and anchor the objects to the scene again. We ask for a new plan only if this fails. We isolate this concept by taking a fixed plan and moving objects in the scene while executing. When displacing objects by $10$\,cm we were still able to complete $66.7\%$ of trials with scene-diff verification and layered recovery, and we did not have to change the plan. Without practicing on the tasks being tested or using ground-truth object poses from the simulator during rollouts, we test our full system (with one open-weight 27B planner with 4-bit weights) on LIBERO-PRO. Under this protocol SPARK achieves a mean success of $75.1\%$ across the 6 position and task settings and $69.7\%$ on the held-out language and object settings. We also achieve a $68\%$ success rate across 11 physical task-embodiment settings on 3 different robots. Our work demonstrates that for an important class of failures, the robot does not need to ask for a new plan for its task. It can be enough to update the system's view of the world. Code and videos can be found at https://cwru-aism.github.io/spark-page/</p></details> | 30 pages, 7 figures |
| **[D&A: Decoupling Description and Assessment Enables VLMs as Zero-Shot Robotic Reward Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03622v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Vision-Language Models (VLMs) hold promise for zero-shot video task-progress estimation in robot learning, yet existing frameworks frequently suffer from confirmation bias, hallucinating progress in failed executions due to implicit success assumptions or relative temporal ranking designs. Naive prompting of frontier models collapses into a binary success detector that fails to capture nuanced, non-linear progress and failures. While post-training approaches can mitigate this through explicit training, they demand expensive real-world robotics data and require computationally intensive post-training for every individual base model. To resolve these limitations, we introduce D&A (Describe & Assess): our approach decouples progress estimation into an objective, task-grounded textual description of scene dynamics followed by an assessment phase using explicit rubrics. By compelling the VLM to synthesize an explicit, text-based definition of absolute success before scoring, the framework anchors evaluations to an external, global criterion rather than the video's internal timeline. Empirical results demonstrate that our method calibrates zero-shot estimates and mitigates confirmation bias, providing fine-grained, partial progress metrics that accurately penalize failed executions while correctly interpolating successful runs. Being purely prompt-based, D&A generalizes across tasks, embodiments, and benchmarks---matching or surpassing post-trained reward models---and its reward estimates enable efficient downstream reinforcement learning.</p></details> | <details><summary>Websi...</summary><p>Website at https://weirdlabuw.github.io/dna</p></details> |
| **[Mediating Human-Swarm Interactions Through a Robot Helper: A Shepherding-Inspired Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03548v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This work investigates human-swarm interaction (HSI) for steering large collectives through an intermediary robot helper. While prior robot-swarm control and HSI techniques commonly assume swarm members are addressable, these assumptions often fail for ``ungovernable'' swarms such as wildlife flocks and schools of fish. Motivated by pastoral shepherding, we compare low-level teleoperation to a higher-autonomy motion-primitives interface in a virtual reality (VR) shepherding task under varied swarm cohesion dynamics. Results indicate that motion primitives that operate on collective shape (e.g., boundary-based control) improve task performance and reduce perceived workload.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at IROS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Exploring Proximity and Tactile Sensing for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03489v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Pre-touch sensing, or sensing information about an object before contact, is an attractive modality for robot hands as it can allow a robot to anticipate and respond to a contact before it happens. In this work, we introduce ToFTouch, a multimodal tactile sensor combining three multizone (4$\times$4 resolution) time-of-flight sensors with an optics-based pressure sensing array. We mount this sensor on a parallel jaw gripper and demonstrate its utility on three distinct tasks: (i) pre-grasp adjustment, (ii) item extraction from clutter, and (iii) item insertion into tight spaces. For pre-grasp adjustment, ToF feedback reduced cycle time by 7.4$\%$ by enabling anticipatory gripper closure during approach. For extraction, ToF sensing enabled successful retrieval across all tested depth offsets ($\pm$2 cm) deviating from the nominal pick point, including conditions where baseline approaches failed, with standard deviations below 0.4 cm. For insertion into dense clutter, we explore the proximity and tactile information provided from ToFTouch during an insertion trajectory. The results demonstrate the utility of multizone ToF sensing in a manipulation environment.</p></details> |  |
| **[OpenZilo: An Open Smart-Ring Platform for Human-Centric Sensing and Interaction with Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03410v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Wearable robot interfaces must connect user input to timely physical action. We present the proposed platform, an open smart-ring platform for studying this connection across task stages. The ring integrates a microphone, an inertial measurement unit, photoplethysmography, and skin-temperature sensing. Its software architecture separates acquisition, event interpretation, interaction policy, and robot execution. We characterize seven operating modes and evaluate speech and gesture control with 10 participants. Across five commands and 250 trials per modality, speech and gestures achieved task success rates of 90.4% and 88.8%, respectively. Recognition accuracy exceeded task success for both modalities. Gaussian hidden Markov models achieved higher accuracy than a support vector machine across three vocabulary sizes, with longer scoring times. Following a shared spoken task start, gesture-based adjustments had shorter median response times than speech. Whole-task success was 70.0% with gestures and 64.0% with speech. We further examine physiological-event-triggered robot interaction using simulated event notifications. Mean notification-receipt-to-arrival times were 11.71 s for the robot and 9.20 s for an on-site person. These descriptive findings support evaluating wearable robot interfaces through both recognition and physical outcomes, with timing measures appropriate to each task stage.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 5 figures, 8 tables</p></details> |
| **[CoSMO: Conformal Semantic Mapping for Open-World Safe Robot Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18321v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Safe autonomous navigation requires not only geometric obstacle avoidance but also semantic awareness. Different object categories demand different safety clearances, and the set of relevant categories may expand as the robot explores novel environments. Vision-language models (VLMs) offer rich semantic representations for building maps of unknown scenes, yet their confidence scores are poorly calibrated, making raw predictions unreliable for safety-critical planning. Conformal prediction (CP) can provide coverage guarantees, but existing methods assume a fixed label set and stationary data, limiting their use in open-world settings. We propose Conformal Semantic Mapping for Open-World Safe Robot Navigation (CoSMO), a semantic safety mapping framework that transforms VLM embeddings into online-calibrated coverage sets and safety-aware obstacle dilations under distribution shift. By calibrating directly in the embedding space, CoSMO avoids the score rescaling that arises in confidence calibration as the vocabulary expands. Experiments on the Matterport3D (MP3D) dataset show that CoSMO reduces semantic collisions by 61.6% relative to a geometry-only baseline, while maintaining formal coverage guarantees even under delayed expert feedback. Real-world experiments on a quadruped robot further demonstrate generalization by achieving long-run empirical coverage of 90.73% against the 90% target. Project website: https://yangming911.github.io/cosmo-page/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the 10th Conference on Robot Learning (CoRL), 2026</p></details> |
| **[Towards end-to-end co-design for one-shot printable task-specific soft robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03216v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Soft robot co-design methods can jointly optimise morphology and control, but the resulting designs are often difficult to manufacture, while manufacturable approaches typically restrict actuator placement or require manual assembly. We present a simulation-based co-design framework that jointly optimises material distribution, pneumatic actuator placement and orientation, and control, while producing designs that can be fabricated monolithically using fused deposition modelling. The framework uses a differentiable 2D plane-stress Material Point Method simulator, incorporating an experimentally validated material model and an empirically derived surrogate model of a 3D-printed inflatable actuator. We demonstrate gradient-based optimisation soft robotic arms for prescribed tip trajectories. Optimised designs are transferred directly from simulation to one-shot monolithic FDM fabrication, with integrated actuators and no post-assembly. Physical experiments demonstrate that the resulting arms reproduce the behaviour predicted in simulation. This framework provides a path from task-level objectives to physically realisable soft robots while retaining substantial freedom over both morphology and actuation.</p></details> |  |
| **[Robot-Factored World Models via Robot Rendering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22535v2)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Action-conditioned video world models predict future observations from an initial observation and an action signal. We propose robot-factored world models (RoFacto), which move two robot-specific factors outside the world model to separate action realization from scene-response prediction. First, action realization: each command sequence is rolled through the robot's own controller and kinematics without scene contact into a deployment-available nominal trajectory that does not require future realized states. Second, robot rendering: this nominal trajectory is rendered through the robot URDF, factoring the robot's geometry, kinematics, and appearance out of the model and into explicit rendered robot geometry. Paired end-effector and scene depth provide geometric cues for contact and occlusion, while camera-aware static RGB context and rendered robot geometry form a shared visual interface across viewpoints and embodiments through which the model learns scene responses. Our experiments show that the rendered interface outperforms vector-conditioned baselines, supports transfer to unseen robot embodiments, and generates robot manipulation videos from retargeted human demonstrations.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://bjkim95.github.io/rofacto/. Camera-ready version for CoRL 2026</p></details> |
| **[A Tightly Coupled Magnetic-Inertial SLAM System for Field Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03106v1)** | 2026-11-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Field robots operating in mines, tunnels, and disaster areas require reliable localization despite GNSS denial and degraded camera and LiDAR measurements caused by dust, smoke, or poor illumination. Missions involving both ground and aerial robots further motivate complementary sensing modalities that do not depend on visibility, preinstalled infrastructure, or wheel contact. This letter presents MISLAM, a tightly coupled magnetic-inertial simultaneous localization and mapping system using an IMU and a nine-magnetometer array, without relying on external odometry or platform-specific motion assumptions. The system combines the inertial navigation model with local polynomial magnetic-field models for short-term motion estimation and a global Gaussian process model for drift correction using an error-state Kalman filter. Offline evaluation on three indoor datasets collected with a handheld rig shows reductions in total endpoint closure error of 45.7% - 78.0% relative to magnetic-inertial odometry. Under poor illumination, MISLAM maintains tracking after visual-inertial odometry fails, achieving a horizontal closure error of 0.332 m, although vertical drift remains a limitation. These results support magnetic-inertial SLAM as a complementary localization modality for robotic sensor suites operating under degraded visual conditions.</p></details> |  |
